By the time evening rolls around, I have these headaches and my whole upper body is just stiff... almost like I've been hauling heavy lab equipment around all day, even when I haven't been. So, yeah, definitely a lot of tension and headaches.\n\nDoctor: Has something traumatic happened to you recently or in the past that you think might be contributing to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. Um, I haven't been through anything traumatic, not recently and not when I was younger, either. My life has been pretty normal in that sense. I haven't had any major accidents or terrible events happen to me... so I don't think it's tied to anything like that. It's really just this constant stress about everyday things going wrong, but it's not coming from a specific bad memory or trauma.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about being judged or evaluated by others, such as your colleagues or family members?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I care about doing a good job at the lab, but I'm not really worried about what my colleagues think of me as a person, or anything like that. It's not about being judged. It's more just... worrying that the experiment itself will fail, or that I'll mess up a calculation and ruin the project. Same with my family, I don't feel like they're evaluating me. The worry is just about the situations themselves, not about people judging me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your life or daily routine recently?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, there was a pretty big change at work. Um, this has all been going on for about seven months now. That's when our lab had a major funding shift, and we entered into a new commercial partnership. It basically changed how everything was being run from top to bottom. So, yeah, my daily routine definitely changed a lot right around then, and that's when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the funding shift and new partnership at your lab is the main cause of your current worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it definitely triggered it, I think. But I wouldn't say it's just about the funding shift anymore. Like, that's when the stress really ramped up, but now I'm just worrying about everything. It's not just the lab or the new partnership. It's... it's my health, it's my family, it's whether I'm going to get into a car accident on the way to work. It's just this constant dread that isn't really tied to that one change anymore, even if that's what started it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these constant worries?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned a minute ago, it's been about seven months. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because my mind is just constantly jumping around, and I can't seem to stop it. I work in a lab, and I'm always worrying about whether our new partnership is going to cut my position, or if my data is wrong. Then it just spirals into stressing that my bills are going to pile up, or that my family thinks I'm falling behind. I even worry that I've already completely ruined my chances at grad school. It just never stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or get rid of?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, the worries I just told you about definitely repeat a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm trying to fight them off or ignore them. It's more like I just get stuck analyzing them because I'm stressed they're actually going to happen. So, no, I don't really have thoughts that I'm actively trying to push away or get rid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go into the lab every day, and I still do everything I'm supposed to do. I wouldn't say I avoid anything. It's just that while I'm doing those things, my brain is just... constantly running through all the things that could go wrong. It just never stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you try to ignore or get rid of?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, the worries I just told you about definitely repeat a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm trying to fight them off or ignore them. I haven't had any major accidents or terrible events happen to me... so I don't think it's tied to anything like that. It's really just this constant stress about everyday things going wrong, but it's not coming from a specific bad memory or trauma.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about being judged or evaluated by others, such as your colleagues or family members?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, the worries I just told you about definitely repeat a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm trying to fight them off or ignore them. It's more like I just get stuck analyzing them because I'm stressed they're actually going to happen. So, no, I don't really have thoughts that I'm actively trying to push away or get rid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go into the lab every day, and I still do everything I'm supposed to do. I wouldn't say I avoid anything. It's just that while I'm doing those things, my brain is just... constantly running through all the things that could go wrong. So I show up, but I'm just incredibly stressed out the whole time I'm there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, trembling, or nausea along with your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I do get headaches. Um, it's mostly because my shoulders and neck just stay so tight all day long. Like, I'll be looking over spreadsheets at the lab and I'll suddenly realize I'm completely clenching my jaw. By the time evening rolls around, I have these headaches and my whole upper body is just stiff... almost like I've been hauling heavy lab equipment around all day, even when I haven't been. So, yeah, definitely a lot of tension and headaches.\n\nDoctor: Has something traumatic happened to you recently or in the past that you think might be contributing to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. Um, I haven't been through anything traumatic, not recently and not when I was younger, either. My life has been pretty normal in that sense. I haven't had any major accidents or terrible events happen to me... so I don't think it's tied to anything like that. It's really just this constant stress about everyday things going wrong, but it's not coming from a specific bad memory or trauma.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about being judged or evaluated by others, such as your colleagues or family members?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I care about doing a good job at the lab, but I'm not really worried about what my colleagues think of me as a person, or anything like that. It's not about being judged. It's more just... worrying that the experiment itself will fail, or that I'll mess up a calculation and ruin the project. Same with my family, I don't feel like they're evaluating me. The worry is just about the situations themselves, not about people judging me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your life or daily routine recently?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, there was a pretty big change at work. Um, this has all been going on for about seven months now. That's when our lab had a major funding shift, and we entered into a new commercial partnership. It basically changed how everything was being run from top to bottom. So, yeah, my daily routine definitely changed a lot right around then, and that's when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the funding shift and new partnership at your lab is the main cause of your current worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it definitely triggered it, I think. But I wouldn't say it's just about the funding shift anymore. Like, that's when the stress really ramped up, but now I'm just worrying about everything. It's not just the lab or the new partnership. It's... it's my health, it's my family, it's whether I'm going to get into a car accident on the way to work. It's just this constant dread that isn't really tied to that one change anymore, even if that's what started it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these constant worries?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned a minute ago, it's been about seven months. It started right around the time the lab got that new partnership, and it's just been going on every day since then.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely are. I mean, it's not just my free time, it's everything. Like, I used to go to these art nights, but I just... I can't even get myself to show up for them anymore. And it's bleeding into my work and home life too. I actually missed an internal deadline at the lab recently because I just couldn't get my thoughts together to organize my writing and review the data. Now, even just doing the normal stuff, like putting together routine reports or trying to cook dinner for my family... it all just feels like way too much to handle. I just can't keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's caused any big arguments or anything, if that's what you mean. Um... like I said, I'm not really seeing my friends at those art nights anymore, so I guess I'm just more isolated from them right now. And with my family, I'm definitely more distracted and struggling to do things around the house, but... no, I wouldn't say it's ruined my relationships. They're mostly just kind of dealing with me being overwhelmed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, it's making it hard to do my job, and I'm missing out on things I used to enjoy. It just feels like I'm constantly stressed and struggling to keep up with everyday life, so... yeah, I'd say it's definitely bringing my quality of life down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you're not worried about potential problems or negative outcomes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm trying to think of something, but... no. It feels like I'm worrying about pretty much everything right now. There isn't really a part of my life that feels safe from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. At work... well, I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my head straight to organize my writing and do the data review. It's gotten to where even routine responsibilities, like just preparing standard reports, feel like way too much to handle. \n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. Like I mentioned before about skipping those art nights... just showing up for things, or even basic stuff like cooking for my family... I just can't keep up with any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or places that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really any specific places. I mean, I'm staying home a lot more, like I mentioned with skipping those art nights, but it's not because I'm afraid of the place itself. It's just... I'm so overwhelmed by everything that I don't have the energy to go out. But it's not like I'm actively avoiding the grocery store or the lab because of a specific fear of being there. So, no, nothing specific like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The worry is just about the situations themselves, not about people judging me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your life or daily routine recently?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, there was a pretty big change at work. Um, this has all been going on for about seven months now. That's when our lab had a major funding shift, and we entered into a new commercial partnership. It basically changed how everything was being run from top to bottom. So, yeah, my daily routine definitely changed a lot right around then, and that's when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the funding shift and new partnership at your lab is the main cause of your current worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it definitely triggered it, I think. But I wouldn't say it's just about the funding shift anymore. Like, that's when the stress really ramped up, but now I'm just worrying about everything. It's not just the lab or the new partnership. It's... it's my health, it's my family, it's whether I'm going to get into a car accident on the way to work. It's just this constant dread that isn't really tied to that one change anymore, even if that's what started it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these constant worries?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned a minute ago, it's been about seven months. It started right around the time the lab got that new partnership, and it's just been going on every day since then.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely are. I mean, it's not just my free time, it's everything. Like, I used to go to these art nights, but I just... I can't even get myself to show up for them anymore. And it's bleeding into my work and home life too. I actually missed an internal deadline at the lab recently because I just couldn't get my thoughts together to organize my writing and review the data. Now, even just doing the normal stuff, like putting together routine reports or trying to cook dinner for my family... it all just feels like way too much to handle. I just can't keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's caused any big arguments or anything, if that's what you mean. Um... like I said, I'm not really seeing my friends at those art nights anymore, so I guess I'm just more isolated from them right now. And with my family, I'm definitely more distracted and struggling to do things around the house, but... no, I wouldn't say it's ruined my relationships. They're mostly just kind of dealing with me being overwhelmed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, it's making it hard to do my job, and I'm missing out on things I used to enjoy. It just feels like I'm constantly stressed and struggling to keep up with everyday life, so... yeah, I'd say it's definitely bringing my quality of life down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you're not worried about potential problems or negative outcomes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm trying to think of something, but... no. It feels like I'm worrying about pretty much everything right now. There isn't really a part of my life that feels safe from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. At work... well, I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my head straight to organize my writing and do the data review. It's gotten to where even routine responsibilities, like just preparing standard reports, feel like way too much to handle. \n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. Like I mentioned before about skipping those art nights... just showing up for things, or even basic stuff like cooking for my family... I just can't keep up with any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or places that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really any specific places. I mean, I'm staying home a lot more, like I mentioned with skipping those art nights, but it's not because I'm afraid of the place itself. It's just... I'm so overwhelmed by everything that I don't have the energy to go out. But it's not like I'm actively avoiding the grocery store or the lab because of a specific fear of being there. So, no, nothing specific like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worries most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I really can't. I try to, you know, just tell myself to focus on one task... like finishing a report or checking an assay for work. But it just doesn't work. One worry just sets off another one, and pretty soon I'm just sitting there staring at my screen. My head just starts running through every single possible way things could go wrong, and I can't stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, it's not just my free time, it's everything. Like, I used to go to these art nights, but I just... I can't even get myself to show up for them anymore. And it's bleeding into my work and home life too. I actually missed an internal deadline at the lab recently because I just couldn't get my thoughts together to organize my writing and review the data. Now, even just doing the normal stuff, like putting together routine reports or trying to cook dinner for my family... it all just feels like way too much to handle. I just can't keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's caused any big arguments or anything, if that's what you mean. Um... like I said, I'm not really seeing my friends at those art nights anymore, so I guess I'm just more isolated from them right now. And with my family, I'm definitely more distracted and struggling to do things around the house, but... no, I wouldn't say it's ruined my relationships. They're mostly just kind of dealing with me being overwhelmed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, it's making it hard to do my job, and I'm missing out on things I used to enjoy. It just feels like I'm constantly stressed and struggling to keep up with everyday life, so... yeah, I'd say it's definitely bringing my quality of life down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you're not worried about potential problems or negative outcomes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm trying to think of something, but... no. It feels like I'm worrying about pretty much everything right now. There isn't really a part of my life that feels safe from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. It's gotten to where even routine responsibilities, like just preparing standard reports, feel like way too much to handle. \n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. Like I mentioned before about skipping those art nights... just showing up for things, or even basic stuff like cooking for my family... I just can't keep up with any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or places that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really any specific places. I mean, I'm staying home a lot more, like I mentioned with skipping those art nights, but it's not because I'm afraid of the place itself. It's just... It basically changed how everything was being run from top to bottom. So, yeah, my daily routine definitely changed a lot right around then, and that's when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the funding shift and new partnership at your lab is the main cause of your current worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it definitely triggered it, I think. It's just this constant dread that isn't really tied to that one change anymore, even if that's what started it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these constant worries?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned a minute ago, it's been about seven months. It started right around the time the lab got that new partnership, and it's just been going on every day since then.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely are. I mean, it's not just my free time, it's everything. Like, I used to go to these art nights, but I just... I can't even get myself to show up for them anymore. And it's bleeding into my work and home life too. I actually missed an internal deadline at the lab recently because I just couldn't get my thoughts together to organize my writing and review the data. Now, even just doing the normal stuff, like putting together routine reports or trying to cook dinner for my family... it all just feels like way too much to handle. I just can't keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's caused any big arguments or anything, if that's what you mean. Um... like I said, I'm not really seeing my friends at those art nights anymore, so I guess I'm just more isolated from them right now. And with my family, I'm definitely more distracted and struggling to do things around the house, but... no, I wouldn't say it's ruined my relationships. They're mostly just kind of dealing with me being overwhelmed all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, it's making it hard to do my job, and I'm missing out on things I used to enjoy. It just feels like I'm constantly stressed and struggling to keep up with everyday life, so... yeah, I'd say it's definitely bringing my quality of life down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you're not worried about potential problems or negative outcomes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm trying to think of something, but... no. It feels like I'm worrying about pretty much everything right now. There isn't really a part of my life that feels safe from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are making it hard for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. At work... well, I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my head straight to organize my writing and do the data review. It's gotten to where even routine responsibilities, like just preparing standard reports, feel like way too much to handle. \n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. Like I mentioned before about skipping those art nights... just showing up for things, or even basic stuff like cooking for my family... I just can't keep up with any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or places that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really any specific places. I'm so overwhelmed by everything that I don't have the energy to go out. But it's not like I'm actively avoiding the grocery store or the lab because of a specific fear of being there. I try to, you know, just tell myself to focus on one task... like finishing a report or checking an assay for work. But it just doesn't work. I'm always thinking about what's coming next, or what could go wrong later today, tomorrow, next week. It's like my brain is always ten steps ahead, just... bracing for the worst possible outcome. I don't really sit around dwelling on the past, if that's what you mean. It's all just... what if this happens, what if that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are worse than what most people would worry about in the same situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really do.
